1. Recognition: Digital Cities 
MAYOR HARTKE  invited members of Team Chandler, which was recognized as a Digital Cities  
award winner for its work in technology innovation, data -driven operations, and inclusive 
technology.  
 

Page 2 of 39 
 
 
KERSTIN NOLD, IT Chief Technology Officer, introduced the IT team and supporting departments 
and explained that the Digital Cities award recognizes effective use of technology to serve 
residents. She highlighted Chandler’s recognition for initiatives such as the implementation of a 
new enterprise r esource planning system to improve efficiency. Ms. Nold emphasized the city’s 
commitment to privacy and cybersecurity, noting that additional technological improvements are 
planned.

MEGAN CRAIGHEAD, Chandler, AZ, stated that at the previous month’s Planning and Zoning 
Commission meeting, a traffic study had been requested, and asked whether the study had been 
conducted. 
 
MAYOR HARTKE asked Kelly Schwab, City Attorney, if that had been presented.  
 
KELLY SCHWAB, City Attorney, said a traffic study was not done.   
 
MS. CRAIGHEAD proceeded with her comments and shared that neighborhood meetings had 
taken place, along with a Planning and Zoning meeting where residents voiced their concerns 
about the proposal development. She noted ongoing worries about the increased traffic it would 
bring to Basha Road, explaining that the roadway was formerly Alma School Road before a new 
alignment was created because the original road could not support heavy traffic.  She added that 
the historic canal and the existing palm trees make it difficult for drivers to see when turning onto 
Basha Road, creating safety concerns for both motorists and pedestrians who use the road to 
access the nearby park. According to her, these concerns have been raised repeatedly but have 
not been addressed by the developers.  Ms. Craighead referenced an immersive experience 
hosted by the City of Chandler in October, where residents were shown the city’s vision for future 
housing and business development. She stated that the proposed project does not reflect that 
vision, describing it instead as a continuation of  high-priced stucco homes that do not align with 
the ideals recently presented to the community.  She concluded that the project, as currently 
designed, does not benefit the city, and does not contribute to the kind of community Chandler 
should be striving to build. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO asked if city staff could come forward and present.  
 
DANA ALVIDREZ, Engineering Services Administrator, shared the following slide.   
• Traffic 
o Original Land Uses:  
 960 daily Trips  
 160 in the AM peak hour 
 138 in the PM peak hour 
o Proposed Land Use:  
 1,074 daily trips  
 79 in the AM peak hour  
 106 in the PM hour  
Page 5 of 39 
 
 
o Expected to add 114 daily trips  
 Equates to 1.2% of the capacity of Basha Road  
 
MS. ALVIDREZ reported that last year’s traffic counts show Basha Road operating at 23.5% of its 
capacity, and the proposed development is expected to add less than 2% more. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO noted that the numbers differed from a previous presentation.  
 
MS. ALVIDREZ explained that the updated figures come from a November traffic study, which 
includes volumes for both the former warehouse and office data not included in the May study.   
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO asked for confirmation that the impact is lower.  
 
MS. ALVIDREZ confirmed it is.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO added that  C ouncil had discussed monitoring traffic after the 
development is built to determine whether mitigation is needed.  
 
MS. ALVIDREZ confirmed staff will monitor impacts on the surrounding roadway network as 
development occurs.  
 
VICE MAYOR ELLIS asked about the remaining capacity.  
 
MS. ALVIDREZ stated that approximately 74% of the roadway capacity remains available.  
 
MONIQUE FULMER, Chandler , AZ, shared her concerns regarding the proposed housing 
development. She noted that although she expected the project would likely be approved, she felt 
it was important to voice her perspective. Having lived in Chandler on and off throughout her life, 
Ms. Fulmer expressed pride in the city and emphasized the importance of honoring its agricultural 
roots. She noted that she does not believe the development reflects that heritage.  Ms. Fulmer 
acknowledged that the builder plans to install a plaque honoring Eddie B asha and his 
contributions to Chandler. She described Mr. Basha’s generosity and highlighted his impact on the 
community. Ms. Fulmer concluded that a neighborhood of homes does not honor Chandler’s 
agricultural history or Eddie Basha’s legacy. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO requested that the applicant provide a project timeline, noting 
neighbors' concerns about extended delays between approval and construction. 
 
BRENNAN RAY, Certified Real Estate & Zoning Attorney, Ray Law Firm, for K. Hovnanian Homes, 
stated that the developer is under contract to acquire the land and would begin construction as 
soon as permits are secured. He explained that the developer has invested approximately 
$750,000 in the preparation of  building and civil improvement plans.  If C ouncil approves the 
Page 6 of 39 
 
 
project, they hope to close on the land and start site work by July, followed by horizontal and then 
vertical construction, including demolition. 
 
VICE MAYOR ELLIS referenced a letter from the Basha family regarding the historical value of the 
land and Eddie Basha’s legacy, noting that the family felt he had been well represented and 
acknowledged in Arizona and Chandler. She asked for clarification for those who had not attended 
the prior meeting. 
 
MR. RAY stated that David Basha, son of Eddie Basha, submitted a letter on behalf of the Basha 
family, which was  forwarded to C ouncil. The letter acknowledged the longstanding partnership 
between the City of Chandler and the Basha family. The family emphasized that Eddie Basha had 
been honored in many ways. Regarding the proposed development, the Basha family stated that 
they feel well represented and support the project. They also support the planned m onument 
signage, which incorporates design elements inspired by the original Basha stores  and serves as 
a tribute to Eddie Basha’s legacy.

29.  Purchase of Small Water Meter Replacement and Field Verification Services 
Move City Council approve Agreement No. PW5 -4640-4871, Amendment No. 1, with D&B 
Construction Group, LLC, and Metering Services, Inc., for small water meter replacement 
and field verification services, in a combined amount not to exceed $1,500,000, for the term 
of January 1, 2026, through December 31, 2026. 
  
LARISSA GRUBER, 2938 E. Teakwood Pl., Chandler, AZ, expressed opposition to the new water 
meter system. Ms. Gruber noted widespread concern, citing lawsuits in Gilbert and other states 
over inaccurate readings, unexplained spikes, and system failures that have financially burdened 
families. She stressed that in a desert environment, accurate water measurement is  critical and 
called for complete transparency and independent verification to ensure the meters function 
properly, with taxpayers protected from potential costs. 
 
MAYOR HARTKE asked City Manager John Pombier to comment on how Chandler’s water systems 
differ from those in other cities and towns.  
 
Page 10 of 39 
 
 
JOHN POMBIER, City Manager, responded that Chandler has been using the same meters for 
approximately 15 years and has conducted extensive reviews to ensure accurate water billing. He 
noted that the city conducts independent testing and monitors anomalies to promptly address 
any unusual readings. Mr. Pombier emphasized that Chandler is committed to providing reliable 
customer service and accurate billing, aiming to prevent the issues seen in other communities.

Consent Agenda Motion and Vote  
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS moved to approve the Consent Agenda items 1-35 of the December 11, 
2025, Regular City Council Meeting, with an additional provision for item 9. The amendment adds 
language to condition seven of Ordinance No. 5144, requiring the developer to provide a written 
Page 11 of 39 
 
 
disclosure statement for each buyer. The disclosure statement must acknowledge that the 
subdivision is located adjacent to or near the Snedigar Sports Complex and Dog Park and may be 
subject to noise and odor impacts, including landscaping equipment, heavy machinery, barking 
from the dog park, sports field lighting, and other effects typical of a public park and sports 
complex; Seconded by Vice Mayor Ellis. 
 
Motion carried unanimously (7-0).

0046 Price Road Innovation Campus, rezoning an approximately 40-acre site from Planned

38.  Resolution No. 5957, Authorizing a Development Agreement between the City of Chandler 
and BA Price Owner LLC for the Redevelopment of Property Located at 3380 South Price 
Road into an Artificial Intelligence Data Center and Tech Park 
Move City Council to consider its position regarding Resolution No. 5957, authorizing a 
Development Agreement between the City of Chandler and BA Price Owner LLC to facilitate 
the redevelopment of property located at 3380 South Price Road into an advanced artificial 
intelligence ("AI") data center and associated Tech Park employment buildings. 
 
MAYOR HARTKE introduced Item 37 and acknowledged the many residents who had reached out 
or attended to share their views, noting that community input is essential to the Council’s decision-
Page 13 of 39 
 
 
making. He asked attendees to keep comments respectful. Mayor Hartke explained that items 37 
and 38 would be considered together.  
 
DAVID DE LA TORRE, Planning & Urban Design Senior Manager , presented the following 
presentation.  
• PLH24-0046 Price Road Innovation Campus Rezoning & Preliminary Development Plan  
• Location:  
o 3380 S. Price Road (approximately 40 acres at Southwest corner Price and Dobson 
roads)  
o Request: Rezone to Planned Area Development (PAD) for a data center and Mid-Rise 
Overlay up to 90 feet in addition to knowledge -intensive uses, advance business 
services and ancillary commercial 
o Preliminary Development Plan (PDP) approval for site layout and building 
architecture 
• Data Center Ordinance  
o Section 35-2214 Zoning Code (adopted Dec. 2022) 
o Data centers as a primary use not permitted unless explicitly  approved as part of a 
PAD  
o Ancillary data centers are permitted in any zoning district where 
industrial/employment uses are allowed as long as:  
 They occupy no more that 10% of building footprint  
 They are used to serve the enterprise functions of the on- site business-not 
data storage and/pr processing services to 3rd parties 
 They are not housed in a stand-alone structure 
• Development Proposal  
o Phase I:  
 Building A (data center) 422,877 sq ft.  
 Building B (R&D/Flex/Office) 50,753 sq. ft.  
• Development Proposal 
o Phase II: 
 Building E (R&D/Flex/Office) 42,674 sq. ft.  
 Building F (R&D/Flex/Office) 42, 674 sq. ft.  
• Development Proposal 
o Phase III:  
 Building C (R&D/Flex/Office) 66,476 sq. ft.  
 Building D (R&D/Flex/Office) 71, 420 sq. ft.  
• Development Proposal  
o Data center is the only tenant identified at this time  
o Proposed zoning condition limits data center to the southwest corner of the site 
(Building A)  
o Mid-Rise Overlay allows up to 90 feet building heights over the entire site  
• Building Architecture  
o Data Center: Tilt up concrete with glazing masonry and metal accents material  
Page 14 of 39 
 
 
• Building Architecture 
o Building B: Tilt up concrete with glazing masonry and metal accent materials  
• Building Architecture 
o Building E: Tilt up concrete with glazing masonry and metal accent materials  
• Neighborhood Outreach 
o Three neighborhood meetings:  
 Feb 26th (9 neighbors attended) 
 Mar. 3rd (12 neighbors attended)  
 Aug. 28th (7 neighbors attended) 
o Notification was provided in accordance with Zoning Code requirements 
o Emails received: 8 in support 200+ in opposition  
• Planning and Zoning Commission Recommendation Oct. 15, 2025 
o Staff recommended denial based on inconsistency with the General Plan  
o P&Z Commission voted 5-1 to approve subject to conditions  
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO asked to return to the slide showing the site overlay and requested 
clarification about the substation located just south of Building A.  
 
MR. DE LA TORRE confirmed the location.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO asked whether the power lines would be placed underground.  
 
MR. DE LA TORRE responded that the applicant has indicated all power lines leading to the site 
will be underground.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO inquired about the approximate acreage the substation would 
occupy. 
 
MR. DE LA TORRE stated that he did not have the exact dimensions available and suggested the 
applicant may be able to provide that information.  
 
MICAH MIRANDA, Economic Development Director, provided an overview of the development 
agreement outlining the performance obligations for the proposed AI data center and 
accompanying tech park. He explained that the agreement requires the data center to use 
mechanical or closed -loop cooling systems only, with water use capped at the Tier 1 allocation 
under the sustainable water use agreement. No water -based cooling may be used after the 
certificate of occupancy unless it is in a closed-loop system. Existing water connections to cooling 
towers must be removed and capped during decommissioning, and thereafter , the site may be 
served only by 1-inch water meters. Mr. Miranda noted that demolition of existing buildings must 
begin within 90 days after approval or within five days of power being disconnected. Demolition 
must be completed within 18 months. Construction of Tech Park Buildings E and F must begin by 
January 2031, or the developer will incur penalties of $200,000 per year; Buildings C and D must 
begin by January 2032 or face $225,000 per year in penalties. All electric lines serving the site must 
Page 15 of 39 
 
 
be placed underground. To safeguard city revenues, the agreement ensures that property tax and 
TPT revenues cannot decrease due to state or federal incentives; future payments must remain 
at the highest level and adjust for inflation. Employment requirements include a minimum of two 
full-time employees per 1,000 square feet, with a median wage of at least $75,000. Jobs must be 
maintained for five years after each building receives its certificate of occupancy. Annual reporting 
is required, and the developer  will be assessed $2,000 per missing job per year if employment 
falls below the minimum.  The facility must be used exclusively for AI and advanced computing 
data center operations; traditional enterprise data centers, colocation services, and cloud storage 
facilities are prohibited. Mr. Miranda concluded by noting that the development agreement has a 
10-year term with an automatic 10-year renewal. 
 
MAYOR HARTKE asked for an explanation of the power arrangements with SRP. 
 
MR. MIRANDA reported that the applicant must enter a formal contract with SRP, expected to be 
finalized in late fall or early December. That agreement will define utility needs and the phased 
delivery of power to the site, anticipated around 2031–2032. 
 
MAYOR HARTKE requested clarification regarding incorrect media reports on water.  
 
SIMONE KJOLSRUD, Water Resources Manager, explained that the project uses closed- loop 
cooling, which requires very little water. Confusion in reporting may have stemmed from the 
standard application language used for unidentified future tenants in shell buildings. She clarified 
that the data center would be limited to about 48,000 gallons per day, and the remaining five tech-
park buildings would be limited to roughly 30,000 gallons per day (about 5,000–6,000 per building), 
unless they later requested more water under the city’s sustainable allocation policy. 
 
MAYOR HARTKE noted the site is restricted to a one -inch water line, which physically caps the 
maximum deliverable water. 
 
MS. KJOLSRUD explained that a one-inch water meter can only deliver roughly 50,000 gallons per 
day, depending on the specific meter, and the site cannot physically receive more than that. If the 
developer ever sought additional water capacity, they would need to return to Council, request a 
larger meter, and undergo the city’s sustainable allocation process. 
 
MAYOR HARTKE clarified that such a request would be a Council decision, not a staff-level decision. 
 
MS. KJOLSRUD confirmed that it would indeed require Council approval. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS asked what the total water allocation for the site would be under 
standard business use and whether a different type of business would require a similar, greater, 
or lesser amount.  
 
Page 16 of 39 
 
 
MS. KJOLSRUD responded that the allocation reflects the c ity’s standard water allowance for any 
commercial or industrial business. The maximum allocation is 48,000 gallons per day, which is 
likely more than a data center would require due to its closed-loop cooling system. This maximum 
is based on the building’s square footage and represents a typical water use level for a building of 
that size in Chandler. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS asked whether Chandler residents subsidize business water use. 
 
MS. KJOLSRUD explained that the c ity conducts a cost -of-service study every two years for each 
category of water users. Water rates are set based on the impact each user category has on the 
system, and each category pays accordingly. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS asked whether any new business connected to Chandler’s 
infrastructure would affect residents’ water rates in the future. 
 
MS. KJOLSRUD explained that all water users fund the maintenance of existing infrastructure . 
Costs for aging pipes are included in the city’s cost of service. Any new or special infrastructure 
required by a development would typically be addressed through a development agreement. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS asked about the site’s current water usage.  
 
MS. KJOLSRUD stated that the site currently uses approximately one million gallons per month 
due to existing evaporative cooling systems for a vacant building. Under the proposed 
redevelopment, those systems would be removed, and new mechanically based, closed- loop 
cooling systems that use little to no water would be installed.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO stated that he was reviewing Development Agreement UDM- 121 
and sought clarification on the water use figures, noting an average annual daily use of 48,631 
gallons and a maximum use of 97,262 gallons, and asked what those numbers represented.  
 
MS. KJOLSRUD explained that the figures came from the applicant’s submission estimating water 
use for the data center. She stated that both numbers represent maximums under the c ity’s Tier 
One sustainable water allocation. The city requires two figures: average daily water use calculated 
across the entire year, and maximum daily use representing peak demand. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO confirmed that the figures were gallons per day and asked whether 
the average was based on 365 days. 
 
MS. KJOLSRUD confirmed and explained that the 48,000 -gallon figure reflects total annual use 
divided by 365, while the 97,000-gallon figure reflects a possible single-day peak, with lower usage 
balancing the annual average. 
 
Page 17 of 39 
 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO noted that multiplying 48,000 gallons by 365 would exceed 12 
million gallons annually and questioned whether that implied higher water use. 
 
MS. KJOLSRUD clarified that the proposed closed- loop mechanical system is expected to use far 
less water than the stated average. The 48,000 -gallon figure represents the maximum allowable 
daily allotment, not the anticipated actual usage, and establishes a  cap that the applicant cannot 
exceed. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER POSTON asked whether staff had estimated the economic impact of the 
project. 
 
MR. MIRANDA responded that an economic impact analysis had been completed. He stated that 
the direct fiscal impact to the c ity over ten years  was approximately $10.2 million, with an 
additional $10.4 million attributable to indirect and induced impacts.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER POSTON asked for clarification on the difference between direct and indirect 
impacts.  
 
MR. MIRANDA explained that direct impacts result from the project’s business operations, while 
indirect impacts reflect secondary economic activity generated by employees and related 
spending. He noted that the difference between the figures is relatively small because data 
centers employ few people and generate limited ancillary spending. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER POSTON asked how the development agreement addressed power supply. 
 
MR. MIRANDA stated that the agreement requires the applicant to secure sufficient power to 
operate the data center. By January 2027, the applicant must have a signed development and 
construction agreement with SRP to deliver 230 -kilovolt power lines to the site. Failu re to do so 
would allow the city to terminate the agreement and revert to prior zoning. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER POSTON asked about the cost of the electrical infrastructure. 
 
MR. MIRANDA said there were no firm estimates, but high -level figures from SRP indicated costs 
exceeding $200 million for the electrical component of the project.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER POSTON asked whether an upfront payment was required.  
 
MR. MIRANDA responded that to remain in SRP’s planning process, the applicant must meet 
certain milestones, including providing a letter of credit and a deposit, which would most likely be 
due in February 2026.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER POSTON expressed concern about the short review timeline for the 
development agreement and asked whether the SRP process warranted the advanced schedule. 
Page 18 of 39 
 
 
MR. MIRANDA stated that the applicant wished to move quickly, likely due to SRP’s timeline. He 
added that the agreement was complex and required staff coordination. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER POSTON asked whether the cited economic impact figures included the 
applicant’s power infrastructure investment. 
 
MR. MIRANDA clarified that the $10.2 million figure reflected direct revenue for  the city, not total 
economic activity. He stated that total economic activity generated by the project was estimated 
at approximately $997 million, exceeding $1 billion when direct, indirect, and induced impacts are 
included, and that this did not include SRP capital expenditures. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER POSTON asked about distinctions between an AI data center and a traditional 
data center. 
 
MR. MIRANDA said the development agreement includes clear definitions, noting that AI data 
centers focus less on storage and more on time-sensitive processing and latency considerations.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER POSTON asked whether power demands could affect the city’s ability to recruit 
businesses to the Price Corridor. 
 
MR. MIRANDA responded that infrastructure capacity is always a consideration, but the Price 
Corridor has approximately 80 megawatts of available power. Any user requiring more than 10 
megawatts would be required to undergo an SRP cluster study. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER POSTON confirmed that the user, not Chandler residents, would bear 
infrastructure costs. 
 
MR. MIRANDA affirmed that SRP’s policy requires the developer  to pay for its own infrastructure 
and generation. 
 
MAYOR HARTKE asked whether SRP conducts cluster studies approximately every six months. 
 
MR. MIRANDA stated that this aligned with his understanding, though he had not seen 
documentation specifying the frequency of cluster study applications. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS asked about the construction timeline and impacts on  the Price 
Corridor, citing current traffic congestion and concerns about effects on existing businesses. 
 
MR. MIRANDA stated that if the project moves forward, SRP will install new 230 -kilovolt lines 
parallel to the existing ones. He displayed the tentative alignment of the power lines and explained 
that in 2020–2021, the city, SRP, and Intel partnered to install the first 230-kilovolt line to serve the 
Intel expansion. The line originates at the Schrader Station and runs underground through an 
existing conduit to the Intel substation. To serve this project, SRP would install an additional 
Page 19 of 39 
 
 
conduit, requiring open- trench work along the power line corridor to Chandler Heights Road, 
followed by reopening the roadway to extend the line to Chaparral and Lake Drive. He noted that 
the second conduit has already been installed, so no additional trenching or major infrastructure 
work would be required beyond temporary closures for line installation, which would be less 
disruptive than prior construction between Schrader Station and Alma School Road and Lake 
Drive. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS asked for clarification on whether the alignment passed through 
residential areas or businesses and for identification of the landmarks shown. 
 
MR. MIRANDA provided a detailed explanation of the map.  He explained that the initial  line will 
run down the power lines right through the power line easement along the railroad tracks. Then 
it will go down to Chandler Heights , up, and then right onto Alma School. Once it intersects Lake 
Drive and Alma School, the second conduit line is already in place. The  connection at Lake Drive 
will then be pulled underground in the existing conduit lines. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS asked about the expected timeline for that segment of construction. 
 
MR. MIRANDA responded that no firm timelines exist, but SRP anticipates construction occurring 
around 2031–2032, following planning, design, engineering, and equipment procurement. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO asked whether the project would require new conduit or could use 
the existing conduit. 
 
MR. MIRANDA explained that along Chaparral and Lake Drive, the Intel improvement line already 
included a pre-installed second conduit, which this project could use. A new  conduit would only 
be required along sections outside Chaparral and Lake Drive, including Alma School, Chandler 
Heights, and the railroad east corridor. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO noted that the original project had planned the conduit for future 
expansion and redundancy. He then asked when the substation would become operational. 
 
MR. MIRANDA stated that there are no definite timelines. Once contracts between the applicant 
and SRP are signed, SRP will begin its procurement process, anticipated for 2031–2032. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO noted that it is six to seven years from now. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER HAWKINS asked whether using the existing conduit at the Alma School 
intersection for this project would limit future expansion or if there is capacity to add more lines 
later. 
 
MR. MIRANDA responded that if additional power were needed beyond the capacity of the existing 
and proposed 230 -kilovolt lines, an alternative route would be required. He noted that the 
Page 20 of 39 
 
 
corridor is largely fully utilized, as underground 230-kilovolt lines require wide easements due to 
heat generation, making it unlikely that another line could be added along the current route.

COUNCILMEMBER POSTON raised concerns about the project’s water use, citing claims that AI 
data centers require significantly more water due to higher energy demand, and asked city staff 
to clarify whether this was accurate.  
 
MR. BACH stated that the claim was inaccurate and added that the development agreement would 
prohibit such water use even if it were true. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER POSTON asked city staff to address the issue.  
 
MS. KJOLSRUD explained that there is some truth to the concern, depending on where water use 
occurs. She stated that the claim is not accurate with respect to Chandler’s water resources. She  
noted that the Salt River Project (SRP) uses water to generate power. She explained that SRP’s 
power generation is primarily hydropower, which is considered very sustainable. SRP releases 
water from dams to spin turbines and generate electricity  and uses a system of pumping water 
during periods of low energy demand and releasing it during periods of higher energy demand . 
As a result, while water is used to generate additional power, it does not come from Chandler’s 
water supplies. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ENCINAS questioned the project's long -term goals , noting the strong 
commitment to job creation and penalties if targets are not met, and asked about the strategy 
and timeline for creating those jobs. 
Page 25 of 39 
 
 
JEFF ZYGLER, Founder and CEO of Active Infrastructure, stated that Jones Lane, the South 
International brokerage firm has been marketing this property for use, but they will continue to 
work with the Chandler economic development team to attract tenants to the property.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER ENCINAS asked whether there was a general sense of what type of tenant 
might be located at the site. 
 
MR. ZYGLER responded that two potential tenants had previously expressed interest but 
ultimately chose other locations because of the lengthy approval process. He stated that one 
tenant relocated to Mesa, while the other’s location was unknown. He added that one was an 
automated manufacturing center and the other a biopharmaceutical company. 
 
VICE MAYOR ELLIS raised concerns regarding the applicant’s involvement in the Chandler 
community. She noted apprehension that the applicant did not live in Chandler and questioned 
the level of care and commitment to the community, particularly given that the investment 
originated from outside the city. She stated that she intended  to provide Mr. Zygler  with an 
opportunity to address these concerns on public record and asked him to explain his position on 
community involvement in Chandler. 
 
MR. ZYGLER responded that his company, Active Infrastructure, was founded with a community -
centric focus. While he resides in New York, he stated that he spends significant time in Chandler. 
He explained that buildings acquired by his company were made available to the Chandler and 
Mesa police and fire departments for training prior to demolition, with hundreds of officers 
regularly using the facilities. He noted that he personally paid for public safety equipment.  Mr. 
Zygler further stated that his company seeks to support community needs proactively. He added 
that his personal phone number would be displayed on company buildings so residents could 
report concerns directly, citing a prior redevelopment project in Delaware as an example of  
responsible community stewardship. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO asked for clarification on how much water the data center would 
use per day. 
 
MR. ZYGLER said the original application was based on a 2 -inch water meter, but the project has 
since been reduced to a 1 -inch meter. He estimated that the maximum allowable flow would 
average about 12,000 gallons per day, with a possible peak of 25,000 gallons. He stated that the 
data center is expected to use approximately 5,000 gallons per day for domestic purposes. 
 
MS. KJOLSRUD added that staff initially expected a 2-inch meter and that the 48,000-gallon figure 
reflected the maximum allowed to remain within the Tier One allocation, not a projection of actual 
water use. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO reiterated his question, asking directly how much water the data 
center would use per day. 
Page 26 of 39 
 
 
MR. ZYGLER stated that the data center is expected to use 3,000 to 5,000 gallons of water per day. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO asked for an explanation of what an AI data center is and why it is 
important to the city. 
 
MR. ZYGLER explained that while the CyrusOne campus totals 168 megawatts, about five times 
larger than the proposed facility, the single building would provide roughly 80 percent of that 
computing power by consolidating operations into a dense footprint. He emphasized the 
importance of proximity to fiber infrastructure for reliability and redundancy, noting that high -
performance operations, such as automated automotive systems, require latency unde r two 
milliseconds, which cannot be achieved from distant locations. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO inquired about the maximum distance to maintain that speed. 
 
MR. ZYGLER said fiber follows existing conduits rather than straight lines, and the maximum 
practical distance is approximately 13 kilometers  (about 8 miles). He noted that amplifying the 
signal can extend the reach, but it also introduces some degradation, and the acceptable distance 
depends on client requirements. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO asked how long construction would take. 
 
MR. ZYGLER replied that the data center would take roughly 24 months to build, though permitting 
and long lead times for electrical equipment could extend the timeline. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO asked how the project would operate in the years before full power 
was available. 
 
MR. ZYGLER said the company has committed not to use on -site generation. He explained that 
utility supply is evolving and that while the project will connect to a 69 kV line with a future 230 kV 
line providing redundancy, achieving full capacity may take several years. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO asked about the risk of losing customers during that period. 
 
MR. ZYGLER said there is strong demand for the facility. While the projected timeline may currently 
indicate six years to full capacity, changes in other regional projects could free up additional power 
sooner. Once approvals and delivery certainty are secured, the project will move forward. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO said he was evaluating the proposal from a broader economic 
perspective, noting concerns raised by analysts about potential overinvestment and a possible AI 
industry bubble. He emphasized the public trust placed in the Council. He questioned how a multi-
billion-dollar investment would be repaid over the next several years without near-term revenue, 
asking how creditors would be paid during the extended build-out period. 
 
Page 27 of 39 
 
 
MR. ZYGLER responded that the capital would be deployed over the construction timeline, with 
revenue beginning once the facility becomes operational, at which point debt repayment would 
commence. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO pressed whether creditors would wait several years for repayment 
and highlighted the high upfront costs for power infrastructure and construction before any 
income is generated. 
 
MR. ZYGLER acknowledged the scale of the investment.  
 
MR. BACH objected, stating that inquiries into financing structure and investment backing were 
beyond what is appropriate for a land-use hearing. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO ac knowledged the point but expressed concern based on past 
projects that failed to materialize after approval. He said his primary concern was not water, noise, 
or other mitigable impacts, but the lack of a clear and viable business case.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS asked about the applicant’s community engagement and outreach. He 
noted that previous meetings had very low attendance, approximately nine participants at one 
session and around twelve at another , whereas current engagement levels appeared much 
higher. He asked the applicant to explain the apparent disconnect between the initial public 
meetings and the current level of community interest.  
 
MR. BACH explained that three mailings, totaling about 1,000 notices, were sent to the public. He 
noted that after the Planning Commission, additional mass mailings circulated beyond the 
project’s original area, which sparked broader interest.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS said he had reviewed 192 pages of materials and expressed concern 
that initial engagement was limited. He noted that the project affects city infrastructure and 
impacts all residents. He asked whether additional outreach to a wider area would be appropriate 
before bringing the project to C ouncil to address concerns and build broader community 
understanding.  
 
MR. BACH said he raised valid points and acknowledged that additional outreach was possible. He 
explained that the project timeline is mainly driven by SRP -imposed deadlines , which limit  
opportunities for broader engagement. Increased outreach would have been preferable without 
those constraints.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS said he understood the applicant’s timeline but emphasized that the 
city’s responsibility is to prioritize residents and their concerns over business schedules. He stated 
that voters’ voices must come first and that, given the level of concern expressed, it was difficult 
for him to support moving forward at this time.  
 
Page 28 of 39 
 
 
MR. BACH responded that Councilmember Harris’  concerns were noted and agreed that the 
project warranted outreach beyond the minimum requirements. He said his role was to present 
a land -use case based on facts and acknowledged C ouncilmember Harris’ duty to represent 
constituents. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS reiterated that his role was to amplify resident voices and ensure 
quality of life, regardless of the size or nature of the concern. He urged the applicant to pause, 
engage more broadly with the community, and return with a proposal that better reflects pu blic 
input. 
 
MR. ZYGLER agreed with the importance of community concerns but said decisions should be 
based on facts and legally binding commitments. He noted the project does not use water for 
cooling and outlined comparisons showing reduced water use relative to prior operation s. He 
added that the development agreement significantly mitigates risk to the city and that some public 
concerns were based on misinformation circulated beyond the original notice area.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER HAWKINS thanked the presenters for attending and for the information and 
feedback provided, stating that all her questions had been answered. 
 
MAYOR HARTKE clarified that the gallium nitride operation involves approximately 100 jobs, not 
750, and that NXP is not shutting down in Chandler but is discontinuing a specific 5G application. 
He thanked the applicants for their presentation and acknowledged the difficulty of the 
discussion. He then asked for an estimate of the number of jobs associated with the data center 
itself, separate from the broader site development. 
 
MR. ZYGLER responded that the data center would employ approximately 50 to 80 people. 
 
MAYOR HARTKE noted that employment for the remaining portions of the site has yet to be 
determined. 
 
MR. ZYGLER clarified that the 50 to 80 positions are full-time on-site employees and do not include 
ancillary jobs provided by service vendors.  
 
MR. BACH added that the northern site would meet minimum square footage requirements, 
equating to approximately two jobs per 1,000 square feet.  
 
MAYOR HARTKE read comment cards submitted by the audience  and then called for comments 
from the public.

JASON DEMPSEY , 4977 E. Longhorn St., San Tan Valley, AZ , spoke in support of the rezoning 
request to allow construction of a new data center facility. Mr. Dempsey stated that the training, 
certification, and professionalism of union tradespeople ensure the project would be built to the 
highest standards. He cited the benefits of union labor  and highlighted the project’s long -term 
economic benefits. He added that supporting union labor strengthens the local workforce and 
helps build a skilled trades pipeline to support future growth in Chandler.  
 
SARAH COVEY, 2333 E. Southern Ave, Tempe, AZ, shared that she has several concerns about the 
project, including uncertainty about consumer protections under SRP programs and how long 
those protections would remain in place, particularly given the lack of state laws addressing AI -
related consumer impac ts. She also questioned the long -term job security associated with AI 
facilities, noting that increased automation could reduce employment over time. She asked 
whether the projected job creation and a 15-year operational horizon were sufficient justification 
for the city’s investment.  Ms. Covey raised questions about the project’s closed -loop cooling 
system, including whether chemical additives would be used, how wastewater would be handled, 
and whether power generation from the associated substation could affect local air quality.  
 
NOAH-JAMES MARKHAM, Tempe, AZ, spoke in opposition to the proposed AI data center. He 
expressed skepticism about the project’s claimed benefits, stating that he did not believe it would 
provide meaningful value to the community or result in significant local production. Mr. Markham 
also raised concerns about water availability in Arizona, emphasizing the need for conservation 
given limited resources.  
 
DOMONIQUE CARTER, 2828 S. Price Rd., Tempe, AZ, spoke in opposition to the rezoning request. 
He emphasized that the water used by the proposed data center would come from the same 
limited supply that Arizona residents rely on, stressing the need for conservation.  He also raised 
concerns about the facility’s power consumption.  Mr. Carter maintained that the city should 
prioritize projects that directly benefit residents, such as affordable housing and essential 
services.  
 
CHRISTINA PRUM, 677 E Commonwealth Ave., Chandler, AZ, spoke in opposition to the rezoning 
request. Ms. Prum clarified that she supports technology and innovation but raised concerns that 
the proposed data center would limit future job opportunities for Chandler students. She noted 
that data centers typically have low worker density, and the projected 30 –50 full-time positions 
would not provide sufficient local career opportunities over the next decade or two.  
 
ORY BROWN, 1235 W Baseline Road, Tempe, AZ, spoke in opposition to the proposed data center. 
Ms. Brown emphasized that as an SRP customer, the project’s costs would directly affect her and 
her community, as well as residents across the valley who would ultimately bear the utility rate 
increases. She noted that SRP has raised rates nearly every year since 2021, totaling a 24% 
increase. Ms. Brown also expressed concern about the societal impact of AI, citing increased 
screen time for youth and changes to educ ation that prioritize generative AI over traditional 
learning.

PATRICIA PAGLIUCA, 2201 N. Comanche Dr., Chandler, AZ, spoke in opposition to the proposed

LARISSA GRUBER, 2938 E. Teakwood Pl., Chandler, AZ,  raised concerns regarding the proposed

terms of real jobs. She emphasized that residents have clearly expressed their opposition to the 
project.  
 
JOANNAH ZAMORA, 275 W. Frye Rd., Chandler, AZ, thanked Council for providing residents with  
the opportunity to share their opinions regarding the proposed data centers  and thanked 
Councilmembers for asking difficult questions and for seeking comprehensive information to 
make informed decisions. She also addressed the applicants, emphasizing that residents are not 
opposed to development in the city, but that development must make sense. She stated that 
investing in projects that contribute to environmental sustainability and economic stability is 
appropriate, and that decisions should not be driven solely by profit. She concluded by thanking 
Council for supporting the community and stated her opposition to the proposed development. 
 
JULIO LOPEZ, 611 W. Kent Pl., Chandler, AZ , raised concerns about the proposed data center’s 
water usage. He suggested alternative uses for the site, such as converting it into green space, 
and noted that multiple data centers already exist in the area. Mr. Lopez also expressed concerns 
about the negative societal impacts of artificial intelligence.  
 
JEREMIAH BAKOVSZKY, 1622 W. Brooks St., Chandler, AZ, stated that approving the data center 
would be a misguided investment in artificial intelligence. He stated that  AI is threatening artists' 
and workers' jobs.  
 
JALEN WOODWARD, 7135 E. Sunnyside Dr.,  Scottsdale, AZ stated that he believes the proposed 
data center would impact not only Chandler  but the entire v alley and the state. He argued that

explain the project’s full impact. Mr. Woodward raised concerns that , while the applicant 
highlighted projected tax revenue for Chandler, it did not disclose the state -level tax breaks it  
would receive on equipment purchases. He emphasized that the applicant must prove the project 
Page 31 of 39 
 
 
will not pollute the desert, water, or soil. Mr. Woodward expressed concern about claims that the 
developer would pay for new electrical infrastructure, noting that there is no guarantee that SRP 
rates would not increase as a result.  
 
MARK JIONG, 3404 E. BIRCHWOOD Pl., Chandler, AZ, questioned the justification for the proposed 
data center. He noted that data centers generate significantly fewer jobs than comparable 
manufacturing or research  facilities. He also raised concerns about increased resource 
consumption, pollution, and heat generation occurring within close proximity to residential 
neighborhoods. He challenged arguments that existing pollution in the area justifies the project, 
asking why the city would replace more beneficial institutions with a facility that could produce 
equal or greater pollution while creating far fewer jobs and offering limited benefit to most 
residents. He further noted that more than ten data centers already exist in the Price Road 
corridor, making another facility unnecessary and harmful.  
 
ELI GOODMAN, 602 N. Bullmoose Dr., Chandler, AZ, expressed concern that the facility would 
negatively impact residents’ lives. S he noted that energy costs have already risen by about 8%, 
largely due to AI data centers. She emphasized that rising costs directly affect workers like herself. 
She also highlighted the potential labor impacts, noting that even if the data center had minimal 
water or energy impacts, its purpose would still be to replace human labor. She criticized the 
development of AI technologies and automation, such as delivery robots, for displacing workers. 
 
RAXHA BHAGDEV, 2381 W. Indigo Dr., Chandler, AZ, expressed concerns about the transparency 
of water usage by the proposed data center, questioning how the community could verify the 
company’s claims. She also highlighted that SRP’s continued reliance on fossil fuels would 
exacerbate summer temperatures i n the valley.  Ms. Bhagdev questioned the effectiveness of 
employment requirements tied to fines, noting that low penalties provide little incentive to hire. 
She also raised concerns about the proposed office buildings, asking what guarantees there are 
that they will remain vacant and pointing to a perceived disconnect in the project’s planning. 
 
MATT YOUNG, 1682 S. Los Altos Dr., Chandler, AZ, stated that although much of the discussion 
had focused on utilities, noise, and jobs, the matter before the Council was fundamentally a 
rezoning issue. He emphasized that the data center ordinance in question was adopted on 
December 12, 2022, and is neith er outdated nor flawed. He expressed concern that the c ity was 
being asked to bend established zoning rules for a single applicant.  Mr. Young asserted that the 
applicant appeared to be circumventing the prohibition on single -use data centers by proposing 
a “technology campus” with multiple buildings and promised jobs. He noted that he lives in 
Chandler and is familiar with the Price Corridor, where at least four office buildings are currently 
vacant. He questioned the claim that proximity to the data center  would attract workers, noting 
that the corridor is approximately 5 kilometers long. At the same time, the applicant stated that 
employees could be located up to 13 kilometers away to meet data latency requirements. He also 
questioned the viability of attr acting workers, given nearby uses the applicant has described as 
burdensome, including the wastewater treatment plant and fire training center.  
 
Page 32 of 39 
 
 
SEEFA ISLAM, 2402 W. Binner Dr., Chandler, AZ, expressed concern that additional development 
would significantly worsen traffic conditions on a corridor already impacted by construction that 
has lasted more than two years.  Ms. Islam stated that constructing a data center in Chandler 
would further strain infrastructure, increase traffic, raise costs for residents, and place additional 
pressure on the c ity’s limited water resources. She cited projections indicating the facility could 
use nearly 50,000 gallons of water per day, approximately 18 million gallons annually, adding to 
already substantial water demands.  She also raised concerns about public health and 
environmental impacts, stating that data centers would contribute to poorer air quality for 
Chandler residents. She referenced persistent sewage odors along Price Road and Chandler 
Boulevard. She noted that Maricopa County has failed to meet EPA air quality standards in recent 
years, while residents frequently receive air quality alerts.  
 
JEFF WENINGER, 4777 S. Fulton Ranch Blvd., Chandler, AZ, thanked C ouncil for hearing public 
input. He referenced a recent amendment regarding Snedigar Park that would notify future 
residents of potential noise impacts, noting that existing residents near similar facilities are 
already experiencing noise issues without adequate resolution. Mr. Weninger recalled a 2010 vote 
approving Continuum in the Price Corridor, which granted the developer approximately $10 
million in infrastructure assistance and allowed data centers by right across the site. As a result, 
eight data centers currently operate there, with more potentially planned. He stated that this area 
has generated hundreds, if not thousands, of noise complaints from nearby residents. He also 
noted that  proponents originally promised 8,000 to 10,000 jobs, but today there are 
approximately 400 jobs in a designated employment corridor on the site. Mr. Weninger noted that 
the city later revised its data center criteria. He expressed concern that the current proposal does 
not conform to the General Plan approved by voters approximately 10 years ago, particularly 
given that voters are being asked to consider the General Plan again. He stated that approving 
this item would undermine that voter-approved vision.  
 
MARK CAMARENA, 4802 N 15TH Ave., Phoenix, AZ, stated that he attended the meeting in solidarity 
with Chandler residents in opposition to the proposed data center. He asserted that the project 
would negatively impact the land and that Chandler residents have the right to determine how 
their community i s developed.  Mr. Camarena expressed concern that the applicant  failed to 
clearly address environmental impacts or community concerns. He warned that economic 
volatility in that sector could negatively affect Chandler if the c ity commits to this type of 
development. Mr. Camarena concluded by requesting Council prioritize community -focused 
investments and social programs that benefit working-class Chandler residents. 
 
VEAN SIMON III, 2312 W. Sunrise Pl., Chandler, AZ, spoke in support of the proposed data center. 
He emphasized that artificial intelligence is a global industry and that cities must invest in AI 
infrastructure to remain competitive.  Mr. Simon shared his son’s experience in AI, highlighting 
how younger generations are using technology to innovate and create economic opportunities. 
He argued that AI is not a speculative industry and that failing to invest would put Chandler at a 
disadvantage. While acknowledging community concerns, he encouraged the Council to address 
regulatory issues and allow the project to move forward.

ANJ BAILEY, 1619 W. Maplewood St, Chandler, AZ, urged the Mayor and Council to consider the 
long-term impacts of the proposed data center on younger residents and the environment. She 
cited examples from other cities where AI development has caused pollution and community 
harm and noted that data centers disproportionately affect marginalized communities. Ms. Bailey 
emphasized concerns about carbon emissions and high water usage in a desert city, highlighting 
Arizona’s ongoing Colorado River water negotiations. She requested Council prioritize 
environmental sustainability and carefully consider the long -term consequences before 
approving the project. 
 
DERRICK ESPADAS, Tucson, AZ, expressed concern that many data center proposals are structured 
in fragmented stages to avoid full evaluation of their community impacts. He noted that projects 
are often divided into multiple entities to improve appearances for investors, comparing this to 
past corporate accounting failures.  Mr. Espadas cautioned that the c ity should carefully review 
any proposed use of AI by law enforcement or private clients, particularly facial recognition, and 
implement safeguards to prevent misuse. He also questioned claims of guaranteed clients, citing 
strong competition and past failures in nearby communities.  
 
ALEXANDRIA JONES, 1101 N. Monte Vista St, Chandler, AZ, spoke in opposition to the proposed AI

community services that directly benefit residents. Ms. Jones argued that the data center primarily 
transfers community resources to a speculative industry, imposes high energy costs on residents, 
creates few jobs, and offers limited benefits while straining city resources. She asked Council to 
prioritize the long-term wellbeing of Chandler families and a sustainable future over approval of 
the project. 
 
AVERY BREWER, 1600 S College Ave Apt 1, Tempe, AZ, expressed opposition to the proposed data 
center. She raised concerns about the facility’s energy consumption, heat generation, and water 
use, noting that increased demand would largely rely on fossil fuels despite higher user fees. Ms. 
Brewer questioned the developers’ transparency regarding resource use, water consumption, 
client data, and job creation, citing low employment numbers at similar facilities nationwide. She 
also highlighted economic uncertainty in the AI sector, noting that major companies remain 
unprofitable. She concluded that the c ity should prioritize long -term, sustainable decisions that 
are economically, environmentally, and socially responsible, and opposed approval of the data 
center. 
 
SAM FERRANTE, 10460 E. Acacia Dr, Scottsdale, AZ, stated that sewage treatment is beneficial and

Mr. Ferrante opposed the proposed data center, arguing that the resource costs outweigh any 
benefits. He noted that developers’ responses regarding water usage were unclear and 
emphasized that accurate calculations require proper engineering methods, not informal 
estimates. He concluded that approving the project without adequate consideration of 
environmental and community impacts would harm the desert ecosystem.

TERESA WAR NICK, 126 N. Evergreen St, Chandler, AZ, opposed the proposed data center. She

highlighting the need for additional housing and walkable, mixed-use areas where people can live

Corridor for this type of development rather than approving the data center. 
 
YEGOR ZENKOV, 213 S. Windstream Pl, Chandler, AZ, spoke in opposition to the proposed data

that even major companies cannot claim more than an 80% reduction in water use and that details 
about the system remain unclear.  Mr. Zen kov also challenged claims regarding prospective 
customers and latency-critical applications, stating that the examples cited by the applicants  do 
not align with realistic AI workloads for such data centers. He argued that the project’s stated use 
cases are misleading and concluded that the proposed facility is unnecessary given actual 
demand. 
 
ELIZABETH LEE, 3445 E. Hawell, Gilbert, AZ, spoke in opposition to the proposed data center. She

innovation should strengthen communities rather than impose risks on them.  
 
INDIGO ROSS, 3939 W. Windmills Blvd Apt 2095, Chandler, AZ, spoke in opposition to the proposed

deplete family resources, and negatively affect public health. She concluded by affirming  the 
importance of protecting the city’s natural resources and future generations, asserting that 
community well-being should take precedence over profit.

JOSEPH RUSSO, 2090 E. Flintlock Way, Chandler, AZ,  opposed the proposed data center. He

opportunities on the site. He also noted that a recent executive order affecting state oversight of

ALDO SOBERON, 4100 S. Pinelake Way Unit 140, Chandler, AZ, spoke in opposition to the proposed

MICHAEL OEI, 2945 E. Harwell Rd, Gilbert, AZ, spoke in opposition to the proposed data center. He

while data centers have benefited from relatively low rate increases, effectively subsidizing

NATHAN TAYLORTAFT, 695 East Sheffield Avenue, Chandler, AZ, spoke in opposition to the

Action Agenda Items 37 and 38 Motion 
Councilmember Harris made a motion to deny items 37 and 38; seconded by Councilmember 
Orlando.  
 
Council Discussion 
COUNCILMEMBER HARRIS thanked the applicant for bringing this project forward. He felt like this 
data center was not a right fit. He evaluated if the project was  smart, people -first, and voter -
approved. He emphasized that putting people first means protecting residents’ peace, health, and 
quality of life. He commented that voters made their vision very clear in the general plan adopted 
in 2016, where Price Corridor was established as a place of innovation, research, and high-quality 
jobs. He stated that he will stay true to what makes Chandler successful: people first leadership, 
smart growth, and respect for Chandler’s voter-approved plans. 
 
COUNCILMEMBER ORLANDO thanked participants for being civil, professional, and courteous. He 
stated that the developer and the city collaborated on the agreement.  He shared interest in the 
city embracing new technologies. The Price Road Corridor is a building block for research 
companies to develop new technologies that can be manufactured locally as well. He had 
questions regarding the potential customer s of this development that the city worked with the 
applicant to answer. He emphasized the importance of a business case scenario, investigating if 
this development would drive revenue or jobs in Chandler. He stated he is against this 
development due to the lack of guaranteed customers for the parcels and answers regarding the 
investor payoff.  
  
VICE MAYOR ELLIS emphasized Chandler’s background with the technology industry in the local 
economy. She commented that Chandler will move forward with technology responsibly. She 
noted that she intended to listen to constituents and hear their feedback and urged them to show 
up for other causes they care about. Her intention in the negotiation for this development was to 
learn what is the benefit for the city . Vice Mayor Ellis shared the City of Chandler will responsibly 
pursue future investment in growing technologies.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER ENCINAS thanked everyone for engaging with Council and sharing input on the 
item. He said for any proposed item, he weighs the long -term benefits, the potential economic 
opportunities, and the overall impact in Chandler. He supports exploring forward- looking 
industries and opportunities that position Chandler for the future that are the right fit. He 
encouraged everyone to stay informed, engaged, and open as Council continues to shape the next 
chapter of Chandler.  
 
COUNCILMEMBER POSTON thanked the applicant and city staff for answering Council’s questions 
about this project. She weighed the importance of what’s best for Chandler, and appreciated the 
feedback shared by residents.  
 
Page 38 of 39 
 
 
MAYOR HARTKE thanked staff and the applicants for their work on this item. He stated that this is 
not the right site for a data center because it does not align with the city’s current general plan.  
 
Action Agenda Items 37 and 38 Vote  
Motion to deny items 37 and 38 passed unanimously, 7-0.

Agenda Item No.
12825 Study Session - Agenda items 37-38 (Ordinance 5143 and Resolution 5957)
Support/Oppose
Oppose
Comments
Data centers are a disaster for cities. They drive up electricity prices for consumers, even
while the centers generally pay discounted rates. In the long term, data centers provide very
few local jobs. Further, the AI they support is causing mass layoffs in many industries across
the country and in Chandler. Any taxes that data centers might pay won't offset these serious
losses. I hope you'll soundly reject the data center proposals. But if you think data centers
belong here, you must protect the people of Chandler. The owners need to be charged at least
consumer rates for electricity, if not more, and to also pay some sort of permanent, ongoing
fee to be distributed to everyone in Chandler. Such fee would help offset the increase in our
utility bills and the cascading job losses their technology is causing and will cause to our
citizens. I hope and pray you will fight for the security of your city's people and not give
benefits to corporations which don't care about our citizens and don't need our help. Thank
you.

Dear Mayor Hartke,
Please vote NO on the Price Road Innovation Campus Data Center.
Notwithstanding Kryrsten Sinema's claims "that we must continue to 
proliferate AI and AI data centers throughout the country", and that 
"federal preemption is coming”... the truth is very likely much 
different.  It is far more probable that the AI bubble currently feeding 
the frenzied growth of data center development will come to an abrupt 
end.  Worst case, in trying to jump on the bandwagon - Chandler will end 
up with another, even larger "Elevation Chandler" derelict building 
1---
sitting a few miles south of the previous eyesore located at the 
Chandler Fashion Center from 2006-2014.
The Chandler city council should be looking out for the safety and 
welfare of Chandler residents. In particular, ensuring water and 
affordable energy are available over the long term rather than chasing a 
short term influx of cash from construction with limited future jobs 
from a highly automated data center.
Modern data centers do not staff at anything like the same levels as 
semiconductor fabs or other manufacturing facilities currently located 
near the proposed data center.  They are highly automated concerns with 
few employees remaining after startup.  Basically just heat generators 
turning electricity into cyber currency and chat-bots requiring massive 
amounts of water for cooling.
The introduction of yet another massive data center in the Phoenix metro 
area will simply result in higher energy costs for residents (higher 
rates than the data center will pay) and a massive increase in water 
consumption.  The city council should be encouraging the conservation of 
water and energy, not the opposite.
We urge you not to approve the Price Road Innovation Campus Data Center.
Sincerely,
Jeff & Tish Summers
Chandler residents since 1994

City Leaders,
I’m writing to oppose the AI data center proposed at the intersection of Dobson and Price 
Rd.  These are the main reasons I oppose this development.
1.  This data center is adjacent to a very large residential lake system.  Noise will carry 
extensively through this area.  I live in the Peninsula sub division and can easily hear noise 
carried from nearby roads etc. because of the amplification from the lakes.  These data 
centers are known to emit large amounts of noise pollution.  Ocotillo residential community is 
one of the nicest areas of Chandler, a point of pride for the city.  
------
1---
2.  Data centers use large amounts of water and electricity.  We live in a water stressed 
environment and our electricity bills are already rising. We just received an increase of our 
electricity rates from SRP with residential rates rising double what the rate increase was on 
commercial. We should not have to subsidize these large data centers with water and 
electricity prices.  
3. From what I understand, the owner of this a data center will put the responsibility to fill the 
rest of the development with business on the city of Chandler.   This property has been 
vacant for a very long time.  The  responsibility should be on the developer to fill out the 
remaining development and propose that to the city as a package. Not “we want to build this, 
you figure out the rest”.
3. It has been said that these AI data centers could be obsolete in a few years, we could be 
dealing with another vacant building.  
4.  Data centers do not employ many people for the size, amount of resources they use and 
noise pollution they emit.
Please consider the residents of the Ocotillo Community when voting.
Meg Tippin
2094 W Peninsula Cir
Chandler, AZ
(480) 371-7776

The Honorable Mayor and Members of the Chandler City Council
Subject: URGENT - Opposition to Proposed Data Center Development at Price and Dobson Roads
Dear Mayor and Members of the City Council,
- - -
--- -- --- -
1---
We are writing to express our strong opposition to the proposed development of a data center located at Price
and Dobson Roads. As concerned new residents of Chandler, we urge you to vote against this proposal,
which we believe will have significant detrimental impacts on our local community and resources.
We reside at 1991 W Coconino Drive and the proposed development is located approximately 1.5 miles
northwest of our home. 
Our primary concerns are focused on the following key areas:
Environmental and Resource Strain
Data centers are known to be massive consumers of both electricity and water, which is particularly
concerning given our location in a desert environment and the existing pressures on the regional power grid.
Water Consumption: Data centers use millions of gallons of water annually for cooling.  This high demand
will strain our limited municipal water supply and contradict the city's conservation goals.
Energy Consumption: These facilities require immense amounts of electricity (a single large data center can
consume as much power as a small town), potentially leading to a strain on the local power grid and
potentially driving up utility costs for residents.
Quality of Life Impacts
The proximity of this industrial use to our residential areas will severely impact the quality of life for
neighborhood residents.
• Noise Pollution: The necessary cooling systems and backup power generators produce significant,
continuous noise pollution (often described as a low-pitched roar or buzzing). This round-the-clock
noise will disrupt the peace of our neighborhood and negatively affect the well-being of residents.
• Air Quality and Safety: The required diesel backup generators are frequently tested and can emit diesel
exhaust and other pollutants, raising concerns about local air quality and neighborhood health. Safety
concerns also exist regarding large lithium-ion battery installations for backup power.
• Visual Impact: The proposed facility will be a large, industrial structure that is visually incompatible with
the surrounding residential or planned commercial area, negatively affecting the aesthetic character of
our community.
Economic and Community Impact
While proponents may promise economic benefits, the long-term costs often outweigh the gains.
• Property Values: The introduction of a massive, industrial facility with associated noise and visual blight
is widely expected to decrease property values for nearby homes.
• Limited Local Jobs: The majority of jobs created are during the temporary construction phase. The
operational data center requires very few permanent, high-skill jobs that benefit the local Chandler
workforce.
We suggest that the city of Chandler take a lesson from the city of Tucson whose city council unanimously
rejected Project Blue and Amazon’s proposed data center.
In conclusion, the proposed data center presents an unacceptable threat to our valuable community resources,
quality of life, and residential property values. We urge the City Council to prioritize the long-term well-
being of its residents and reject the proposal for the data center at Price & Dobson Roads.
We request that this letter be included in the public record for the upcoming hearing. Thank you for your time
and consideration of our concerns.
Sincerely,
Michael & Donna Kasmin
1991 W Coconino Drive
Chandler, AZ 85248

As a Chandler resident for the past four years, and as a senior citizen who votes in every election, I
am writing with reference to the Price Road Innovation Campus project, intended to establish an AI
data center at 3380 S Price Road (corner of Dobson). I write to voice my strong opposition to this
project. AI data centers require enormous amounts of water to cool the machines--obviously a bad
idea in Arizona. Second, such centers require an enormous amount of power to run and would
place additional pressure on a grid already strained throughout the long Phoenix summers. SRP has
just announced that rates are being increased. Rates will only rise even further over time if a project
like this is given the green light. Communities in other states have complained about the noise and
bright lights (these data centers run around the clock). I was very disappointed to learn that the
Chandler Planning and Zoning Commission voted 5-1 to recommend that the Chandler City
Council approve the project. This is extremely short-sighted. I urge members of the Chandler City
Council to vote to reject this project. Thank you very much for considering these comments.

As a longtime Chandler resident and an environmental researcher, I'm writing to strongly oppose
the approval of the Price Road Innovation Campus for various reasons. A) Data Centers use millions
of gallons of water per year. Why would we build another one in one of the driest places on Earth?
B) Data Centers use up incredible amounts of electricity, adding stress to our local infrastructure
and forcing utilities to continue to rely on fossil fuels, delaying the closure of coal plants, and have
even led to the recommissioning of nuclear power plants just to power these centers. In addition, it
is a known fact that the siting of a Data Center goes hand in hand with increasing electricity costs
for the local populations. Residents of Chandler have already seen rate hikes on their energy bills.
Will you burden us with another one just to satisfy the whims of tech billionaires?C) Data Centers
do not provide an economic benefit to the local population, and the employment they bring is
negligible.D) AI is not real artificial intelligence, they are glorified pattern matching tools. It is
trained by stealing everyones collective data and IP. It doesnt think for itself, it just replicates what
others have done. It has been shown to be biased and replicate the worst traits of humanityE) The
AI boom driving the proliferation of Data Centers is a bubble that is about to burst. It has not been
profitable for anyone and investors are starting to back out. Trillions of dollars have been pumped
into Do you want the next building that goes up on this site to become vacant again?F) Unlike what
the tech bros and former senator Synema would have us believe, this investment in AI is not about
technological advances or national security, the overwhelming majority of the AI technologies out
there are either being used to create AI slop (mindless online content), pornography (in the form of
deep fake porn, virtual ai girlfriends and even child pornography), or is being used to develop
surveillance systems meant to oppress us and rob us of our freedom.G) There are countless studies
that have shown the detrimental effects that AI is having on our society. It is robbing us of our
creativity, critical thinking, writing skills, learning skills and will end up funneling us into one hive
mind at the whims of the oligarchs that have unleashed this technology at a faster rate than any
other technology in the history of humanity.H) Data Centers have been linked withan increase in
health conditions usch as cancers misscarriages, contaminated water, noise pollutiomn and more.
These are just a handful of reasons. This project has received a ton of opposition from the
community. I hope you listen to your constituents.

I strongly oppose the proposed AI Data Center that the Council will be voting on this week.
Restraint is called for when there are already 150 data centers in the state, 140 in Maricopa County
alone. As other parts of our beautiful state are destroyed, often by foreign companies, in the
ongoing search for more copper and other resources, when do the citizens get to choose what
matters? When I moved here in 1991, our city population was around 140,000. It is now
approaching 300,000. As I understand it, we have no more room to move out, so we're now
building up. We are quickly depleting the very resources that keep us alive in Arizona, and too
many people are totally unaware of the damage that projects like this can cause. Chandler must
use restraint in this decision and not fall prey to the lobbyists who have no connection to anything
except their own profit and bragging rights. Please keep our great city liveable - protect our water,
our energy sources, our city services, and our way of life here by voting against this data center.

• Limited local job crea�on: Beyond the ini�al construc�on phase, data centers are highly
automated and create few long-term jobs for local residents. The Price Road Corridor has
always been Chandlers crown jewel for job employment; this use provides minimal jobs.
Please keep Chandler a well-planned city, that is discerning in the projects and development it allows

Hello,
I am writing in opposition of building ANY NEW data centers in Chandler.  As an electrical
engineer in the field of computer chip design, I know that the new AI data centers are not like
the old CLOUD data centers.  The new data centers are heavy consumers of electric power,
much more so than cloud data centers.  They also need more cooling, which will either
require extensive water resources, or even more electricity if they are air cooled.
Furthermore, these new AI data centers do not bring in more jobs, like a large Intel or
Microchip FAB.  They just need the space for their servers and memory farms.  They take up
1---
our land as we reach build-out and give little in return to the city.  
We residents don’t need more noise pollution from the data centers.  We can’t afford the loss
of our water.  And we need the land as our population grows, either for housing or for
businesses that actually bring in jobs.

      Thank you for giving me a few minutes of your time as you consider the construction of a
large data center on Price Rd.  We have been residents of Chandler for 34 years and have
seen much of the growth in this city. This has been a positive, healthy city in many ways, and

keep our community clean and healthy, and not waste water.  Which brings me to the
proposed data center.  
       As time is moving forward quickly in the AI world, we are challenged to keep up so we
do what is good, healthy, and right for God's creation.  As AI seems to be taking over
everywhere, there are more and more questions about how it effects our environment.  I was
reading a journal, Sojourners, which told the story of the terrible misuse of AI to make
-----
1---
children's books, which have turned out to be "flooded with AI "slop" --cheap, slapped-
together picture books written and illustrated entirely by AI"  
       So, what do poor books for our kids have to do with a data center?  Look at this quote
from the story and you will see what we are up against!!
"Generative AI also impacts the environment. It depends on massive data centers that
require a tremendous amount of electricity and water to cool them. A New York Times ar�cle
looked at the eﬀects of a data center for Meta (the parent company of Instagram and Facebook) in

construc�on of the center. The county now faces poten�al water shortages and soaring water costs
as the center, likely consuming as much as 500,000 gallons of water a day, has come online and other
companies seek to build centers nearby. According to The New York Times, “nine companies had
applied to build data centers in Newton County, some asking for as much as six million gallons of
water a day—more than the county’s en�re daily use.”  Sojourners -Nov. 2025.

vote is "NO!" Do not allow this to happen, and don't be threatened by the idea that the Federal
government might step in and make us do it.  We need to see you lead us by following values that
care for all of our city, values that save water, use less electricity, and keep our environment clean
and healthy.  Wait for a project on the Price Rd site that will be be�er for our city and our residents.
